Like if you're not maxed out all the time and have almost entirely GPUdriven workloads, the idle power on here was almost always 20 to 30 watts lower. And other ARM SBC PCs, like ones with Rock Chip chips, are even more efficient and have more bandwidth, too. But ultimately, I didn't do this stuff because it made sense. I did it because it's fun to learn about the Pi's limitations, GPU computing, and PCI Express. And that goal was achieved here. I want to thank MicroEnter for sponsoring this video and Dolphin for letting me borrow a few of their PC Express boards.

## 24. TrueNAS on a custom Raspberry Pi NAS

Joel Zero in the TREST community created a fork of TREST that runs on ARM. So I thought I'd give it a try on a Raspberry Pi. I currently run an Ampear ARM server in my rack with Linux and ZFS as my primary storage server and a Raspberry Pi running ZFS as a backup. My configuration for these ARM NAS is up on GitHub. TRAS basically adds a nice web UI and some other conveniences. So, I've been hoping they'd support ARM for years now, but it seems the sentiment in that community was always ARM servers aren't powerful enough to run serious storage servers despite myself and many others doing so for many years. But that's besides the point. The question you're probably asking right now is why would I put TRASS on a Raspberry Pi? I mean, if you watch the channel, you might not wonder that, but I found numerous times running modern applications that require a lot of hardware on slower hardware is an excellent way to expose little configuration flaws and misconceptions that lead to learning how to run the applications much better on more capable hardware. From my Raspberry Pi Dramble to my Pedabyte Pi project, running apps intended for much more powerful hardware taught me a lot. So, maybe running TRASS would be a fun learning exercise. I've done it on x86 servers, but that's boring. It It's too easy. I don't learn much when a project goes off without a hitch, and I'm not forced to look closely at some of the configuration quirks. One glaring problem, though, on a Raspberry Pi is it doesn't have official support for UEFI, a standard way that you boot computers and interface operating systems to device firmware. Raspberry Pi only officially supports device treebased Linux boot, which is much less standard. That means you can't just install any old Linux distro on the Pi. You have to use ones built specifically for the Pi. There are good OSs for the Pi like Raspberry Pi OS based on Debian, but it's not the same as grabbing any ISO, even Windows, and installing it on like my Amper workstation. To get past this restriction, we have to rely on a community project forked from Windows on Raspberry Pi. Specifically, I'm using number one gets RPI5 UFI fork. To get your Pi 5 to support UEFI, you need to first update the EPROM or the firmware on the Pi to a version later than the June 9, 2025 beta release. You can check what version you're running with the command RPI EPROM update. Typically, to upgrade the EPROM, you could use Raspberry Pi Imager, run apt upgrade, or even use the RPI EPROM update utility, but that'll just get you a May8 version, at least at the time I'm making this video. To get the latest version, you can force your Pi to use a beta version of the firmware. To do that, edit the file in etsy/default/arrpieom update and change latest to beta. Then run pseudo arrom update- a and reboot. Once all that's done, you can boot into UFI. I downloaded the zip file from the RP5 UFI project and copied all the files in it over to my Pi's micro SD card in the boot FS volume. Then I stuck that back in my Pi and booted it up. If you have a monitor plugged in, you should see a standard EDK2 bootloader screen appear. You can follow the on-creen instructions to get into the menu or it'll try booting off any valid discs that you have connected. In my case, I went through the menus and set up some options like the PCI Express bus speed. There are other options also that you can set differently depending on what OS you're trying to install. But after you're done with that, save the settings and reset. Now that the Pi is booting into UFI, you can install TRNAS. Download a TRUNAS on ARM ISO from this website. For my test, I installed 25.04.2. Write the ISO to a USB drive using a tool like Bolina Etcher. Then insert the USB drive into the Pi and boot it up. It should automatically boot into the TREAS installer, but if it doesn't, go into the boot menu and select it manually. At this point, the TRES install should just go off without a hitch. Now, don't try installing it on the micro SD card because you need to keep that setup as the UEFI boot volume. You could install it to another USB drive, an NVME, or whatever else, just not the main micro SD card. When Truness is finished installing, reboot and unplug the installer. And in my case, the first time Truness Scale booted up, it got stuck booting one of the services that was trying to generate TREAS files. I'm guessing it just got held up a little bit since the Pi is a little slower than it's used to. I'm not sure. But to fix that, I logged into the Linux console and started the service that was failing manually. And then after a reboot, it seemed to have no more issues, but it did have an issue using the built-in Ethernet. The UFI firmware for the Pi 5 doesn't support anything through the RP1 chip and that sadly includes Ethernet. So I had to plug in this external USB Ethernet dongle that let the networking work and now Truness could run its web dashboard. From there I could see all the settings. It recognized I was running on a Raspberry Pi, but I think it was a little confused judging by some of the unknowns in the dashboard. Before I try to set up any storage, I should run through the other things that don't work because of the missing RP1 support. You can't use the fan header for PDM fan control. You can't use camera or display connections. And you can't use any GPIO, which is a lot. Andrea Delapora, and I know I'm butchering that name, I'm sorry, is a Seuss developer, and he's working on upstreaming RP1 support into Linux with some help from Raspberry Pi, but progress has been a little bit slow. But that made me wonder, why doesn't Raspberry Pi consider official UFI support in the first place? With or without like Microsoft's official blessing, being able to boot vanilla Windows 11 for ARM on a Pi would be nice. Not to mention any regular ARM Linux DRO, including Truness Scale, would just boot right out of the box. I don't know. But anyway, to test things in the real world, I wanted to see how Truness would run on this thing, the Home Labs Pi storage server, which was sent to me earlier this month and uses a custom CM5 SATA backplane and a 3D printed enclosure for a sixbay NAS. I got TRASS installed on a CM5 Lite in here using the same process as the Pi 5 earlier, but when I installed four SATA hard drives in here, they spun up, but they weren't recognized. right now. In addition to all the other things that aren't working, the UFI support only works with one PCIe device. Since the HomeLabs Pi storage server has a PCIe switch, so it can do 2.5 gig Ethernet and SATA for the hard drives, neither of them are recognized. These devices all worked perfectly out of the box on Raspberry Pi OS, though. So, I quickly switched over to that OS to test out ZFS on this thing, and it works great. I could set up a RAID Z1 array and get around 250 megabytes per second copying over hundreds of files on both my Mac and Windows 11. So, the takeaway is I can't get truness going on this box, but it should work fine on simpler builds that don't rely on a PCI Express switch. But that means four drive NVME hats like this one from Geekpie also won't work with Truness yet because they also rely on switches. You can only do one NVME drive at a time or you could like plug them in over USB. I guess because of the current UFI limitations, I would still recommend running TRES on higherend ARM hardware like my Ampear server. If you want to stick to an SBC though, there is UFI firmware for the RK3588 boards too. It may offer even more functionality than the Pi, but check the compatibility list. You could also just go the boring route and install TREAS on an x86 server where it's fully supported, but where's the fun in that? Until next time, I'm Jeff Gearling.

And yes, both of the flight attendants who walked by asked me about it. Luckily, I think it was more curiosity than suspicion. But anyway, one thing I hadn't thought about before this was how being inside a metal can, the airplane, and having a transponder very close by, which had a very strong signal, I might not be able to set up dump 1090 to see other planes besides the one that I was on. I mean, it was pretty easy to track our own plane, except I had to keep updating the location manually since my laptop didn't have GPS. Every once in a while, a few other aircraft would pop in, but no matter how we held the antenna and I set the gain settings, we couldn't get more than four or five other planes at a time. I think the best thing about using dump 1090 on an airplane was I don't have to watch the dumb chat pop-up cycle through on Southwest's built-in flight tracker. And before switching gears over to Meshtastic, I popped open GQRX to see what the ADSB data looked like on a waterfall. And well, it looks like there are a bunch of well-defined channels for the data. something that maybe we can explore more in the future. But I hopped over to Meshtastic and the main thing I wanted to see is if I could get some long range contacts since I was so high up in the air. The 900 MHz band that Meshtastic uses really likes to have a clear shot between devices. The wavelength is about 33 cm. So walls, trees, pretty much anything with mass will reduce or attenuate the signal. If you go way up in an airplane, well, you basically get a line of sight to a huge part of the country. And with my antenna sitting on the tray table in the middle seat, we could pick up nodes about a 100 miles away from our flight path up in Denver. Unfortunately, since the airplane is a big metal can and we didn't have a window seat, the signal wasn't as good as it could have been if I had stuck the antenna in a window. I sent out a few messages, but besides this one cryptic message that looks like it was garbled, I didn't get any responses. When you're messing with RF, there are so many variables to consider. Like we tried a number of orientations for the antenna and even extended it a bit to see if that made much difference. But I think the biggest issue was I didn't have the window seat so I couldn't get a clear shot down to earth. So I decided to pack up the radio until we got to open source. And after gawking over things like a walking coffee table and this messmerizing infinity mirror thing, we headed to the Meshtastic booth. They had a ton of radios on display and there was a router node mounted high up above the show floor serving everyone at the event. I got to talk to the folks at the booth and found out one of them had set up this neat little telegraph display. You send it a DM on the mesh and it prints it out on the thermal paper right away. But getting down to business, I plugged the hackerf into my laptop and could see the short turbo mesh was really busy here. Every once in a while, I'd see some activity on one of the other frequencies, but mostly there were these tiny blips relaying messages and node data all around the open source show floor. And I noticed at one point something was sweeping across the entire band. That's this thing that's kind of stepping up through the waterfall. Maybe someone from a three-letter agency was trying to scan for illegal transmissions. I don't know. But before I headed off to the rest of the show, I moved over to the Wi-Fi band. And look at how busy that is. There were multiple 2.4 GHz networks. Plus, I'm sure a thousand devices using Bluetooth were probably also polluting the frequencies quite a bit. But with all this noise, I'm just amazed how good modern devices are at getting any connection through at all. Anyway, my main mission to decode people's messages at OpenSauce on the Pi laptop was a bust. I could see the traffic, but I couldn't get anything decoded. notice the top area of the case is basically relying on convection since there's no fan up there and that's where the RAM and the ssdr so I popped off the cover and put a fan right up on top forcing 160 so if you're getting less performance at a higher price then yeah that's kind of a bad deal but let's see and right out of the gate geekbench paints a pretty bleak picture for the pie I mean it's a lot closer to arm lately but to complete the power draw discussion here are the stats for idle power draw and the maximum if you have a little server running 24/7 and you don't need double the that big unless of course you're running off solar or battery power but moving on for the integrated Graphics the Mini PC is still beating the pi pretty handily I couldn't even get gravity Mark to load on the pi finally memory bandwith really shows how the N150 in here is being held back by the slow ddr4 Ram some of these cheap mini PCS have ddr5 but usually it means either switching to soldered on- RAM chips or switching CPUs ultimately though these little mini PCS are a good value despite the toasty insides it's obvious they cut a few Corners in this design to save money and that leaves performance on the table and for the P it's less than a 100 bucks all in if you can sacrifice a couple specs but that's part of my point if you compare the cheapest new Mini PC and the most expensive Pi you're not really comparing Apples to Apples even if they're both running Linux the debate over Raspberry Pi versus tiny PCS isn't as simple as some people think it is value is a complicated beast and there are tons of great deals if you're willing to go used or if you realize you don't even need all the power of a full pie or Mini PC and you can just use an old computer that you already have collecting dust but who am I kidding some people just want to feel validated in their own decision and I get that but it's hard to make a universal Claim about what's the best value unraid traz or whatever else I want on here without much hassle on the hardware side I'm a little nervous about Cable Management especially if it has to be open and closed a few times that fan cable get tons of little features you don't normally get in an ass like full-size pcie and built-in Wi-Fi and who knows maybe I could downgrade this chassis and build the most Overkill raspberry Pine nass coding so it's not as useful as a media server an adm's app store limits you to just the built-in VM and container Solutions which aren't bad but if you want to get more advanced they definitely don't grow with you as you go the proprietary Hardware design means upgrades besides maybe faster drives or more RAM are out of the question because there's no easy way to upgrade the motherboard or CPU this whole box is going to be obsolete in like 10 years I have links to both of these boxes in the description and I'd love to hear what what you think DIY or buy are these just way too expensive or do you think they'll find a niche until next time I'm Jeff Kling
